\max  \left\{\| \psi (x) \| _ {\infty}, \| \psi^ {\prime} (x) \| _ {\infty}, \| \psi^ {\prime \prime} (x) \| _ {\infty} \right\} \leq 2 \nu b _ {p}. \tag {73}
$$

in light of (72) and (71), showing (68) boils down to bounding  $\left|\mathbb{e}\psi^{\prime}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{a}^{\mathrm{t}}\boldsymbol{\beta}}{\nu\sqrt{p}}\right) - \mathbb{e}\frac{\boldsymbol{a}^{\mathrm{t}}\boldsymbol{\beta}}{\nu\sqrt{p}}\psi \left(\frac{\boldsymbol{a}^{\mathrm{t}}\boldsymbol{\beta}}{\nu\sqrt{p}}\right)\right|$ . to proceed, we define for every  $(i,j)$ ,

$$
\rho_ {i j} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \frac {\boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {f} _ {j}}{\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \| ^ {2}} \tag {74}
$$

and

$$
\tilde {a} _ {j, \backslash i} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \sigma \left(\boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {f} _ {j} - \rho_ {i j} \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i}\right) = \sigma \left(\boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \left(\boldsymbol {i} - \boldsymbol {p} _ {i}\right) \boldsymbol {f} _ {j}\right),
$$

where  $\pmb{p}_i = \frac{\pmb{f}_i\pmb{f}_i^\top}{\|\pmb{f}_i\|^2}$  denotes the orthogonal projection onto the 1-d space spanned by  $\pmb{f}_i$ . it is easy to check that  $a_i = \sigma (\pmb{g}^\top \pmb{f}_i)$  is independent of  $\tilde{a}_{j,\backslash i}$  for all  $j\neq i$ . it follows that

$$
\mathbb {e} a _ {i} \psi \left(\frac {1}{\nu \sqrt {p}} \sum_ {j \neq i} \tilde {a} _ {j, \backslash i} \beta_ {j}\right) = \mathbb {e} a _ {i} \mathbb {e} \psi \left(\frac {1}{\nu \sqrt {p}} \sum_ {j \neq i} \tilde {a} _ {j, \backslash i} \beta_ {j}\right) = 0, \tag {75}
$$

where the last equality uses the assumption that  $\mathbb{e}a_{i} = 0$  due to  $\sigma (x)$  being an odd function. applying (75) and after some manipulations, we can verify the following decomposition:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\nu \sqrt {p}} \psi \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\nu \sqrt {p}}\right) \right] - \mathbb {e} \psi^ {\prime} \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\nu \sqrt {p}}\right) \\ = \underbrace {\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\frac {1}{\nu \sqrt {p}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \beta_ {i} a _ {i} \delta_ {i} - 1\right) \psi^ {\prime} \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\nu \sqrt {p}}\right) \right]} _ {\text {(a)}} + \\ \underbrace {\mathbb {e} \left\{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \frac {\beta_ {i} a _ {i}}{\nu \sqrt {p}} \left[ \psi \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\nu \sqrt {p}}\right) - \psi \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\nu \sqrt {p}} - \delta_ {i}\right) - \psi^ {\prime} \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\nu \sqrt {p}}\right) \delta_ {i} \right] \right\}} _ {(b)} , \\ \end{array}
$$

where

$$
\delta_ {i} = \frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\nu \sqrt {p}} - \frac {\sum_ {j \neq i} \tilde {a} _ {j , \backslash i} \beta_ {j}}{\nu \sqrt {p}}. \tag {77}
$$

by stein's identity, when  $\frac{a^{\mathsf{t}}\beta}{\nu\sqrt{p}}$  follows the standard gaussian distribution, the left-hand side of (76) exactly equals to zero. intuitively, this quantity should be approximately equal to zero when  $\frac{a^{\mathsf{t}}\beta}{\nu\sqrt{p}}$  is approximately standard gaussian. this is what we are going to prove next. in what follows, we derive bounds for the two parts on the right-hand side of (76), separately.

we start with part (a). to simplify the notation, we let  $\chi = \frac{1}{\nu\sqrt{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\beta_{i}a_{i}\delta_{i}$ . applying the bound on  $\|\psi'(x)\|_{\infty}$  in (73) gives us

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \text {p a r t} (\mathbf {a}) | \leq (2 \nu b _ {p}) \mathbb {e} | \chi - 1 | \\ \leq (2 \nu b _ {p}) (\mathbb {e} | \chi - \mathbb {e} \chi | + | \mathbb {e} \chi - 1 |) \\ \leq \left(2 \nu b _ {p}\right) \left(\sqrt {\operatorname {v a r} (\chi)} + | \mathbb {e} \chi - 1 |\right), \tag {78} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last step is due to hölder's inequality. it is now clear what to do: to show part (a)  $\rightarrow 0$ , we just need to verify that  $\mathbb{e}\chi \to 1$  and  $\operatorname{var}(\chi) \to 0$ .

calculating  $\mathbb{e}\chi$  is easy. applying the independence property (75), we have

$$
\mathbb {e} \chi = \frac {1}{\nu^ {2} p} \mathbb {e} \big [ \sum_ {i \leq p} \beta_ {i} a _ {i} (\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {\beta}) \big ] = \frac {1}{\nu^ {2} p} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {a} \boldsymbol {\beta},
$$

where  $\boldsymbol{\sigma}_a = \mathbb{e}\boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{a}^{\mathrm{t}}$ . one can show that  $\boldsymbol{\sigma}_a \approx \boldsymbol{\sigma}_b$ , where the latter is defined in (70). specifically, lemma 5 in appendix d gives us

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {a} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {b} \right\| \leq \frac {\left(1 + \kappa_ {p} ^ {4} + \| \boldsymbol {f} \| ^ {4}\right) \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}} \\ \leq \frac {\left(1 + \kappa_ {p} ^ {4}\right) \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

with the second inequality due to (65). recall the definition of  $\nu$  in (70). we then have

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \mathbb {e} _ {\chi} - 1 | = \frac {\left| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {a} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {b}) \boldsymbol {\beta} \right|}{\nu^ {2} p} \leq \frac {(1 + \kappa_ {p} ^ {4}) \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}} \big (\frac {\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| ^ {2}}{\nu^ {2} p} \big) \\ \leq \frac {\left(1 + \kappa_ {p} ^ {4}\right) \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| \operatorname {p o l y} \log p}{\nu^ {2} p}, \tag {79} \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the last step we use a simple inequality  $(\| \beta \| ^2\leq$ $\| \beta \|_{\infty}\| \beta \|_{\sqrt{p}})$  to bring the final bound to a convenient form.

next, we consider the variance term in (78). introducing the shorthand notation  $u_{k} = \pmb{g}^{\mathsf{t}}\pmb{f}_{k}, 1 \leq k \leq p$ , we rewrite  $\delta_{i}$  in (77) as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta_ {i} = \frac {\beta_ {i} \sigma (u _ {i})}{\nu \sqrt {p}} + \frac {1}{\nu \sqrt {p}} \sum_ {j \neq i} \beta_ {j} [ \sigma (u _ {j}) - \sigma (u _ {j} - \rho_ {i j} u _ {i}) ] \\ = \frac {\beta_ {i} \sigma (u _ {i})}{\nu \sqrt {p}} + \frac {1}{\nu \sqrt {p}} \sum_ {j \neq i} \beta_ {j} \left[ \sigma^ {\prime} \left(u _ {j}\right) u _ {i} \rho_ {i j} - \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {\prime \prime} \left(u _ {j}\right) \left(u _ {i} \rho_ {i j}\right) ^ {2} \right. \\ \left. + \frac {1}{6} \sigma^ {\prime \prime \prime} \left(\theta_ {i j}\right) \left(u _ {i} \rho_ {i j}\right) ^ {3}\right) ], \tag {80} \\ \end{array}
$$

where to reach the second equality we have used taylor's expansion, with  $\theta_{ij}$  denoting a point between  $u_{j} - \rho_{ij}u_{i}$  and  $u_{j}$ . substituting (80) into the expression for  $\chi$  leads to

$$
\chi = \gamma + \delta , \tag {81}
$$

where

$$
\begin{array}{l} \gamma = \frac {1}{\nu^ {2} p} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \left[ \beta_ {i} \sigma \left(u _ {i}\right) \right] ^ {2} + \frac {1}{\nu^ {2} p} \sum_ {i \neq j} \beta_ {i} \beta_ {j} \sigma \left(u _ {i}\right) \left[ \sigma^ {\prime} \left(u _ {j}\right) u _ {i} \rho_ {i j} \right. \\ \left. - \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {\prime \prime} \left(u _ {j}\right) \left(u _ {i} \rho_ {i j}\right) ^ {2} \right] \tag {82} \\ \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\delta = \frac {1}{6 \nu^ {2} p} \sum_ {i \neq j} \beta_ {i} \beta_ {j} \sigma \left(u _ {i}\right) \sigma^ {\prime \prime \prime} \left(\theta_ {i j}\right) \left(u _ {i} \rho_ {i j}\right) ^ {3}. \tag {83}
$$

this then allows us to write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sqrt {\operatorname {v a r} (\chi)} = \sqrt {\operatorname {v a r} (\gamma + \delta)} \leq \sqrt {2 \operatorname {v a r} (\gamma) + 2 \mathbb {e} [ \delta^ {2} ]} \tag {84} \\ \leq \sqrt {2 \operatorname {v a r} (\gamma)} + \sqrt {2 \mathbb {e} [ \delta^ {2} ]}. \\ \end{array}
$$

the term involving  $\delta$  on the right-hand side of (84) is easy to bound, even deterministically. using our assumptions about the function  $\sigma_p(x)$  stated in the lemma, namely it has a compact support and bounded third derivatives, we have

$\left|\sigma(u_i)u_i^3\right| \leq \mathrm{polylog}p$  and  $\left|\sigma'''(\theta_{ij})\right| \leq \mathrm{polylog}p$ . in addition, since the feature vectors satisfy (64), we can verify from the definition (74) that  $\max_{i \neq j}|\rho_{ij}| \leq \frac{c\kappa_p}{\sqrt{p}}$  for some constant  $c$ . it follows that

$$
| \delta | \leq \frac {\kappa_ {p} ^ {3} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\nu^ {2} p ^ {5 / 2}} \sum_ {i \neq j} | \beta_ {i} \beta_ {j} | \leq \frac {\kappa_ {p} ^ {3} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\nu^ {2} p}, \tag {85}
$$

where the second inequality is due to the simple bound that  $\sum_{i\neq j}|\beta_i\beta_j|\leq p\| \boldsymbol {\beta}\|_\infty \sum_i|\beta_i|\leq p^{3 / 2}\| \boldsymbol {\beta}\|_\infty \| \boldsymbol {\beta}\|$

now we tackle the more challenging task of bounding  $\operatorname{var}(\gamma)$  in (84). we first note that, since  $u_{i} = \pmb{g}^{\top}\pmb{f}_{i}$  and  $u_{j} = \pmb{g}^{\top}\pmb{f}_{j}$ , we can view  $\gamma$  as a differentiable function of  $\pmb{g}$ , denoted by  $\gamma (\pmb {g})$ , with  $\pmb {g}\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\pmb{i}_d)$ . the gaussian poincaré inequality (see, e.g., [46, theorem 3.20]) then gives us

$$
\operatorname {v a r} (\gamma (\boldsymbol {g})) \leq \mathbb {e} \| \nabla \gamma (\boldsymbol {g}) \| ^ {2}, \tag {86}
$$

where the gradient  $\nabla \gamma (\pmb {g})$  can be computed, with some diligence, as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \nabla \gamma (\boldsymbol {g}) \\ = \frac {1}{\nu^ {2} p} \left(\sum_ {i \leq p} \beta_ {i} ^ {2} q _ {1} (u _ {i}) \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} + \sum_ {i \neq j} \left[ \beta_ {i} q _ {2} ^ {\prime} (u _ {i}) \right] \left[ \beta_ {j} q _ {3} (u _ {j}) \right] \rho_ {i j} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \right. \\ + \sum_ {i \neq j} \left[ \beta_ {i} q _ {2} \left(u _ {i}\right) \right] \left[ \beta_ {j} q _ {3} ^ {\prime} \left(u _ {j}\right) \right] \rho_ {i j} \boldsymbol {f} _ {j} \\ + \sum_ {i \neq j} \left[ \beta_ {i} q _ {4} ^ {\prime} (u _ {i}) \right] \left[ \beta_ {j} q _ {5} (u _ {j}) \right] \rho_ {i j} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \\ \left. + \sum_ {i \neq j} \left[ \beta_ {i} q _ {4} \left(u _ {i}\right) \right] \left[ \beta_ {j} q _ {5} ^ {\prime} \left(u _ {j}\right) \right] \rho_ {i j} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {f} _ {j}\right), \tag {87} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $q_{1}(u) = 2\sigma(u)\sigma'(u)$ ,  $q_{2}(u) = \sigma(u)u$ ,  $q_{3}(u) = \sigma'(u)$ ,  $q_{4}(u) = -\frac{1}{2}\sigma(u)u^{2}$ , and  $q_{5}(u) = \sigma''(u)$ . in light of (86), we just need to show that  $\|\nabla \gamma(\pmb{g})\|$  is properly bounded. we do so by controlling the norm of each term on the right-hand side of (87).

note that our assumptions about the function  $\sigma_p(x)$  implies that  $\| q_i(u)\|_{\infty}\leq \mathrm{polylog}p$  and  $\| q_i'(u)\|_{\infty}\leq \mathrm{polylog}p$  for  $1\leq i\leq 5$ . moreover,  $\| \boldsymbol {f}\| \leq \mathrm{polylog}p$  by assumption. thus, the first term on the right-hand side of (87) can be bounded as

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{\nu^ {2} p} \sum_ {i \leq p} \beta_ {i} ^ {2} q _ {1} (u _ {i}) \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \right\| \leq \frac {\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\nu^ {2} p}. \tag {88}
$$

for the second term, we first rewrite it in the form of a matrix-vector multiplication as

$$
\frac {1}{\nu^ {2} p} \sum_ {i \neq j} [ \beta_ {i} q _ {2} ^ {\prime} (u _ {i}) ] [ \beta_ {j} q _ {3} (u _ {j}) ] \rho_ {i j} \pmb {f} _ {i} = \frac {1}{\nu^ {2} p} \pmb {f d} _ {1} \pmb {m} \pmb {d} _ {2} \beta ,
$$

where  $d_{1} = \mathrm{diag}\left\{\beta_{i}q_{2}^{\prime}(u_{i})\right\}, m = \mathrm{diag}\left\{\| f_{i}\|^{-2}\right\} f^{\top}f - i,$  and  $d_{2} = \mathrm{diag}\{q_{3}(u_{j})\}$ . clearly,  $\| d_1\| \leq \| \beta \|_{\infty}$  polylog  $p$  and  $\| d_2\| \leq \mathrm{polylog}p$ . we can also verify that

$$
\| \boldsymbol {m} \| \leq c \left(\| \boldsymbol {f} \| ^ {2} + 1\right) \leq \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p. \tag {89}
$$

it follows that

$$
\left\| \frac {1}{\nu^ {2} p} \sum_ {i \neq j} \left[ \beta_ {i} q _ {2} ^ {\prime} \left(u _ {i}\right) \right] \left[ \beta_ {j} q _ {3} \left(u _ {j}\right) \right] \rho_ {i j} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \right\| \leq \frac {\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\nu^ {2} p}. \tag {90}
$$

similarly, the fourth term on the right-hand side of (87) can be rewritten as  $\frac{1}{\nu^2p} f\widetilde{d}_1\widetilde{m}\widetilde{d}_2\beta$ , where  $\widetilde{d}_1 = \mathrm{diag}\left\{\beta_iq_4'(u_i)\right\}$ ,  $\widetilde{d}_2 = \mathrm{diag}\left\{q_5(u_j)\right\}$ , and  $\widetilde{m} = m \circ m$ , with  $\circ$  denoting the hadamard product of two matrices. the spectral norm of  $\widetilde{m}$  can be bounded as

$$
\| \widetilde {m} \| \leq \| \widetilde {m} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} = \left[ \sum_ {i \neq j} \rho_ {i j} ^ {4} \right] ^ {1 / 2} \leq c \kappa_ {p} ^ {2}, \tag {91}
$$

for some constant  $c$ , where the last inequality is due to (64). this then allows us to bound the norm of the fourth term of the gradient expression as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \frac {1}{\nu^ {2} \sqrt {p}} \sum_ {i \neq j} \left[ \beta_ {i} q _ {4} ^ {\prime} \left(u _ {i}\right) \right] \left[ \beta_ {j} q _ {5} \left(u _ {j}\right) \right] \rho_ {i j} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \right\| \tag {92} \\ \leq \frac {\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| \kappa_ {p} ^ {2} \mathrm {p o l y l o g} p}{\nu^ {2} p}. \\ \end{array}
$$

the situations for the third and fifth term on the right-hand side of (87) are completely analogous, and thus we avoid the repetitions. with the bounds in (88), (90) and (92), we can now apply (86) to get

$$
\sqrt {\operatorname {v a r} (\gamma)} \leq \frac {(1 + \kappa_ {p} ^ {2}) \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\nu^ {2} p}.
$$

combining this bound with those in (85), (84), (79), we can retrace our steps back to (78) and conclude

$$
| \text {p a r t (a)} | \leq \frac {b _ {p} \left(1 + \kappa_ {p} ^ {4}\right) \| \beta \| _ {\infty} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\mu_ {2 , p} \sqrt {p}}, \tag {93}
$$

where the last inequality also uses the fact that  $\pmb{\sigma}_b\succeq \mu_{2,p}^2\pmb{i}$  and thus

$$
\nu \geq \mu_ {2, p} \| \beta \| / \sqrt {p}. \tag {94}
$$

now the remaining task is to bound the part (b) in (76) before we can complete the proof. using taylor's expansion, we have

$$
| \text {p a r t} (\mathrm {b}) | = \left| \frac {1}{2 \nu \sqrt {p}} \mathbb {e} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \beta_ {i} a _ {i} \psi^ {\prime \prime} \left(\theta_ {i}\right) \delta_ {i} ^ {2} \right|
$$

where  $\theta_{i}$  is some point between  $\frac{\pmb{a}^{\mathrm{t}}\pmb{\beta}}{\nu\sqrt{p}} -\delta_{i}$  and  $\frac{\pmb{a}^{\mathrm{t}}\pmb{\beta}}{\nu\sqrt{p}}$  . by assumption, the function  $\sigma (x)$  considered in this lemma is supported on  $[- \tau_p,\tau_p]$  for some  $\tau_{p}\leq \mathrm{polylog}p$  . we can then write  $a_{i} = \sigma (u_{i}) = \sigma (u_{i})\mathbb{1}_{[-\tau_{p},\tau_{p}]}(u_{i})$  . this step of introducing an indicator function is not strictly necessary, but it helps to simplify some of our later arguments. we now have

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \text {p a r t} (\mathbf {b}) | = \left| \frac {1}{2 \nu \sqrt {p}} \mathbb {e} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \beta_ {i} a _ {i} \psi^ {\prime \prime} \left(\theta_ {i}\right) \delta_ {i} ^ {2} \mathbb {1} _ {[ - \tau_ {p}, \tau_ {p} ]} (u _ {i}) \right| \\ \leq \frac {b _ {p} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \mathbb {e} \left[ \delta_ {i} ^ {2} \mathbb {1} _ {[ - \tau_ {p}, \tau_ {p} ]} (u _ {i}) \right], \tag {95} \\ \end{array}
$$

where to reach the last inequality we have also used (73) and the boundedness of  $a_{i} = \sigma(u_{i})$ . using a similar taylor's expansion as in (80) but only to the second order, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \mathbb {e} \left[ \delta_ {i} ^ {2} \mathbb {1} _ {[ - \tau_ {p}, \tau_ {p} ]} (u _ {i}) \right] \\ = \frac {1}{\nu^ {2} p} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \mathbb {e} \left[ \beta_ {i} a _ {i} + \sum_ {j \neq i} \left(\sigma^ {\prime} (u _ {j}) \tilde {u} _ {i} \rho_ {i j} - \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {\prime \prime} (\theta_ {i j}) (\tilde {u} _ {i} \rho_ {i j}) ^ {2}\right) \beta_ {j} \right] ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\tilde{u}_i\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}u_i\mathbb{1}_{[-\tau_p,\tau_p]}(u_i)$ . expanding the right-hand side of this expression then gives us

$$
| \text {p a r t (b)} |
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \leq \frac {b _ {p} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\nu^ {2} p ^ {3 / 2}} \big (\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| ^ {2} + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \mathbb {e} \big [ \sum_ {j \neq i} \sigma^ {\prime} (u _ {j}) \rho_ {i j} \beta_ {j} \big ] ^ {2} \\ \left. + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \mathbb {e} \left[ \sum_ {j \neq i} \sigma^ {\prime \prime} \left(\theta_ {i j}\right) \rho_ {i j} ^ {2} \beta_ {j} \right] ^ {2}\right) \\ \leq \frac {b _ {p} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\nu^ {2} p ^ {3 / 2}} \left(\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| ^ {2} + \mathbb {e} \| \boldsymbol {m} \operatorname {d i a g} \left\{\sigma^ {\prime} (u _ {j}) \right\} \boldsymbol {\beta} \| ^ {2} \right. \\ + \mathbb {e} \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {m}} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\beta}} \| ^ {2}), \tag {96} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $m, \widetilde{m}$  are the matrices considered in (89) and (91), respectively, and  $\widetilde{\beta} = [|\beta_1|, \ldots, |\beta_p|]^\top$ . using the spectral bounds given in (89) and (91), and the inequality (94), we get

$$
| \text {p a r t} (\mathbf {b}) | \leq \frac {b _ {p} \left(1 + \kappa_ {p} ^ {4}\right) \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\mu_ {2 , p} ^ {2} \sqrt {p}}.
$$

substituting this inequality and (93) into (76), and using the fact that  $\mu_{2,p} \leq \mathrm{polylog}p$ , we are done.

# b. joint distributions

lemma 2 shows that  $\frac{a^{\mathsf{t}}\beta}{\sqrt{p}}$  has an asymptotically gaussian distribution. using this result, we can easily show that the asymptotic distribution of  $\frac{a^{\mathsf{t}}\beta}{\sqrt{p}}$  and  $g^{\mathsf{t}}\xi$  is jointly gaussian, via a conditioning technique.

lemma 3. consider a sequence of activation functions  $\{\sigma_p(x)\}$  and two-dimensional test functions  $\{\varphi_p(x;s)\}$  such that, for every  $p$ ,

1)  $\sigma_p(x)$  is an odd function;  
2)  $\max \left\{\| \sigma_p'(x)\|_\infty ,\| \sigma_p''(x)\|_\infty ,\| \sigma_p'''(x)\|_\infty \right\} \leq \mathrm{polylog}p;$  
3)  $\sigma_p(x)$  is compactly supported. specifically, there is some threshold  $\tau_{p}\leq \mathrm{polylog}p$  such that  $\sigma_p(x) = 0$  for all  $|x|\geq \tau_{p}$ ;  
4)  $\varphi_p(x;s)$  is differentiable with respect to  $x$ . moreover, there is a function  $b_{p}(s)$  such that

$$
\max  \left\{\| \varphi_ {p} (x; s) \| _ {\infty}, \| \varphi_ {p} ^ {\prime} (x; s) \| _ {\infty} \right\} \leq b _ {p} (s). \tag {97}
$$

for any fixed vectors  $\beta \in \mathbb{r}^p$  and  $\xi \in \mathbb{r}^d$  with  $\| \xi \| = 1$ , it holds that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {p} \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\sqrt {p}}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {p} \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {b} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\sqrt {p}}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| \\ \leq \frac {[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {2} (z) ] ^ {1 / 2} (1 + \kappa_ {p} ^ {4}) \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \mathrm {p o l y l o g} p}{\mu_ {2 , p} ^ {2} \sqrt {p}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

here,  $z \sim \mathcal{n}(0,1)$ ,  $\pmb{a},\pmb{b}$  are defined the same way as in lemma 2, and  $\pmb{f} = [f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{p}]$  is a collection of feature vectors satisfying (64) and (65).

proof. to lighten the notation, we will omit the subscript  $p$  in  $\sigma_p(x)$  and  $\varphi_p(x; s)$  in the proof. the key idea in our proof is to rewrite the jointly gaussian random variables  $g^{\mathsf{t}}\pmb{f}$  and

$\pmb{g}^{\mathsf{t}}\pmb {\xi}$  via an equivalent representation through conditioning. it is easy to check that

$$
\left(\boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {f}, \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \stackrel {{\text {l a w}}} {{=}} (s \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {f} + \widetilde {\boldsymbol {g}} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {i} - \boldsymbol {\xi} \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\mathsf {t}}) \boldsymbol {f}, s),
$$

where  $s \sim \mathcal{n}(0,1)$  and  $\widetilde{g} \sim \mathcal{n}(0,\pmb{i}_d)$  are two independent sets of gaussian random variables. let

$$
\rho_ {i} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i}, \quad \widetilde {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {i} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} (\boldsymbol {i} - \boldsymbol {\xi} \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\top}) \boldsymbol {f} _ {i}, \quad \text {a n d} \quad \widetilde {\boldsymbol {u}} _ {i} = \widetilde {\boldsymbol {g}} ^ {\top} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {i}. \tag {98}
$$

we can then redefine the entries of  $\mathbf{a}$  and  $\mathbf{b}$  as

$$
a _ {i} = \sigma \left(s \rho_ {i} + \widetilde {u} _ {i}\right) \quad \text {a n d} \quad b _ {i} = \mu_ {1, p} \left(s \rho_ {i} + \widetilde {u} _ {i}\right) + \mu_ {2, p} z _ {i}, \tag {99}
$$

without changing their probability distributions. the reason we do such decomposition is that  $\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_i$  is independent of  $s$ . this convenient independence structure allows us to calculate the expectations in (98) by first conditioning on  $s$ .

applying taylor's expansion to the expression for  $a_{i}$  in (99), we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} a _ {i} = \sigma (\widetilde {u} _ {i}) + \sigma^ {\prime} (\widetilde {u} _ {i}) s \rho_ {i} + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {\prime \prime} \left(\theta_ {i}\right) \left(s \rho_ {i}\right) ^ {2} \\ = \sigma (\widetilde {u} _ {i}) + \mu_ {1, p} s \rho_ {i} + \left[ \sigma^ {\prime} (\widetilde {u} _ {i}) - \mathbb {e} \sigma^ {\prime} (\widetilde {u} _ {i}) \right] (s \rho_ {i}) \\ + \left[ \mathbb {e} \sigma^ {\prime} \left(\widetilde {u} _ {i}\right) - \mu_ {1, p} \right] \left(s \rho_ {i}\right) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {\prime \prime} \left(\theta_ {i}\right) \left(s \rho_ {i}\right) ^ {2}, \tag {100} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\theta_{i}$  is some point between  $\widetilde{u}_i$  and  $\widetilde{u}_i + s\rho_i$ . this expansion then leads to

$$
\mathbb {e} \varphi \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\sqrt {p}}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) = \mathbb {e} \varphi \left(\frac {\sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \sigma (\widetilde {u} _ {i})}{\sqrt {p}} + \frac {s \mu_ {1 , p} \sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \rho_ {i}}{\sqrt {p}} + \delta_ {1} + \delta_ {2}; s\right),
$$

where

$$
\delta_ {1} = \frac {s \sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \rho_ {i} [ \sigma^ {\prime} (\widetilde {u} _ {i}) - \mathbb {e} \sigma^ {\prime} (\widetilde {u} _ {i}) ]}{\sqrt {p}}
$$

and

$$
\delta_ {2} = \frac {s \sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \rho_ {i} \left[ \mathbb {e} \sigma^ {\prime} (\widetilde {u} _ {i}) - \mu_ {1 , p} + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {\prime \prime} (\theta_ {i}) s \rho_ {i} \right]}{\sqrt {p}}.
$$

using the bounded derivative assumption in (97), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} \varphi \left(\frac {\boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\sqrt {p}}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \mathbb {e} \varphi \left(\frac {\sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \sigma (\widetilde {u} _ {i})}{\sqrt {p}} + \frac {s \mu_ {1 , p} \sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \rho_ {i}}{\sqrt {p}}; s\right) \right| \\ \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ b _ {p} (s) \left(\left| \delta_ {1} \right| + \left| \delta_ {2} \right|\right) \right] \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {2} (s) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \left(\left[ \mathbb {e} \delta_ {1} ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2} + \left[ \mathbb {e} \delta_ {2} ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

next, we show that the terms involving  $\delta_{1}$  and  $\delta_{2}$  in (101) are small.

the quantity  $\mathbb{e}\delta_1^2$  is small due to concentration. to see that, let  $\gamma_{1}(\widetilde{\pmb{g}})\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\left[\sum_{i}\beta_{i}\rho_{i}\sigma^{\prime}(\widetilde{\pmb{g}}^{\top}\widetilde{\pmb{f}}_{i})\right] / \sqrt{p}$ . clearly,  $\delta_1 = s[\gamma_1(\widetilde{\pmb{g}}) - \mathbb{e}\gamma_1(\widetilde{\pmb{g}})]$ . from the independence of  $s$  and  $\widetilde{\pmb{g}}$ ,

$$
\mathbb {e} \delta_ {1} ^ {2} = \operatorname {v a r} \left(\gamma_ {1} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {g}})\right) \leq \mathbb {e} \| \nabla \gamma_ {1} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {g}}) \| ^ {2}, \tag {101}
$$

with the last step being the gaussian poincaré inequality. recall the definition of  $\rho_{i}$  and  $\widetilde{\pmb{f}}_i$  in (98). one can verify that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \nabla \gamma_ {1} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {g}}) \right\| = \left\| (\boldsymbol {i} - \boldsymbol {\xi} \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\top}) \boldsymbol {f} \operatorname {d i a g} \left\{\rho_ {i} \sigma^ {\prime \prime} (\widetilde {u} _ {i}) \right\} \beta / \sqrt {p} \right\| \\ \leq \kappa_ {p} (\| \beta \| / \sqrt {p}) \text {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p}, \tag {102} \\ \end{array}
$$

where to reach (102) we have used the bound  $\max_i|\rho_i|\leq \kappa_p / \sqrt{p}$  due to (64). substituting (102) into (101) then gives us

$$
\left[ \mathbb {e} \delta_ {1} ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2} \leq \kappa_ {p} \left(\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| / \sqrt {p}\right) \text {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p}. \tag {103}
$$

to bound  $\mathbb{e}\delta_2^2$ , we first note that  $\mathbb{e}\sigma'(\widetilde{u}_i) \approx \mu_{1,p}$ . more precisely, a simple bound (139) in appendix d yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} \sigma^ {\prime} \left(\widetilde {u} _ {i}\right) - \mu_ {1, p} \right| \leq \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p \left| \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {i} \| ^ {2} - 1 \right| \\ = \operatorname {p o l y} \log p \left| \| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \| ^ {2} - 1 - \left(\boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i}\right) ^ {2} \right| \\ \leq \kappa_ {p} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p}. \\ \end{array}
$$

this then gives us

$$
\left| \delta_ {2} \right| \leq \left(s ^ {2} + | s |\right) \kappa_ {p} ^ {2} \left(\sum_ {i} \left| \beta_ {i} \right| / p\right) \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p},
$$

and thus

$$
[ \mathbb {e} \delta_ {2} ^ {2} ] ^ {1 / 2} \leq \kappa_ {p} ^ {2} (\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| / \sqrt {p}) \text {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p}. \tag {104}
$$

in light of (103) and (104), the left-hand side of (101) is well under control.

using the equivalent representation for  $\pmb{b}$  in (99), we have

$$
\mathbb {e} \varphi \big (\frac {\boldsymbol {b} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}}{\sqrt {p}}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\xi} \big) = \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {\text {s h i f t}} \big (\frac {\sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \left(\mu_ {1 , p} \widetilde {u} _ {i} + \mu_ {2 , p} z _ {i}\right)}{\sqrt {p}}; s \big),
$$

where  $\varphi_{\mathrm{shift}}(x;s)\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\varphi (x + \frac{s\mu_{1,p}\sum_i\beta_i\rho_i}{\sqrt{p}};s)$  is simply a shifted version of  $\varphi (x;s)$ . combining this with (101), (103) and (104), we can now bound the left-hand side (lhs) of (98) as

lhs of (98)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \leq \left| \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {\text {s h i f t}} \left(\frac {\sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \widetilde {a} _ {i}}{\sqrt {p}}; s\right) - \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {\text {s h i f t}} \left(\frac {\sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \widetilde {b} _ {i}}{\sqrt {p}}; s\right) \right| + \delta_ {3} \\ \leq \mathbb {e} \left| \mathbb {e} \left[ \varphi_ {\text {s h i f t}} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \widetilde {a} _ {i}; s\right) \mid s \right] \right. \\ \left. - \mathbb {e} \left[ \varphi_ {\text {s h i f t}} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \sum_ {i} \beta_ {i} \widetilde {b} _ {i}; s\right) | s \right] \right| + \delta_ {3}, \tag {105} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\widetilde{a}_i = \sigma (\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}^\top \widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_i)$ ,  $\widetilde{b}_i = \mu_{1,p}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{g}}^\top \widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_i + \mu_{2,p}z_i$ ,  $\mathbb{e}[\cdot |s]$  denotes conditional expectation given  $s$ , and the "remainder" term is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta_ {3} = \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {2} (s) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \left(\kappa_ {p} ^ {2} + 1\right) \left(\| \beta \| / \sqrt {p}\right) \text {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p} \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {2} (s) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \left(\kappa_ {p} ^ {2} + 1\right) \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {\infty} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p} \tag {106} \\ \end{array}
$$

note that, for any fixed  $s$ , we can use lemma 2 to control the conditional expectation in the first term on the right-hand side of (105). indeed, with  $s$  fixed,  $\varphi_{\mathrm{shift}}(x;s)$  can be viewed as a one-dimensional test function and it satisfies all the assumptions stated in lemma 2. the only thing that is different here is that we are now using  $\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_i\}$  as the feature vectors. thus, to apply lemma 2, we need to check that this modified set of feature vectors still satisfy the condition in (64). but this is easy to do. recall that  $\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_i = (\boldsymbol {i} - \boldsymbol {\xi}\boldsymbol{\xi}^\top)\boldsymbol {f}_i$

with  $\{\pmb{f}_i\}$  satisfying (64) for some  $\kappa_p = \mathcal{o}(p^{1/8})$ . thus, for all  $i,j$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {i} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {f}} _ {j} - \delta_ {i j} \right| = \left| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {i} - \boldsymbol {\xi} \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\mathsf {t}}) \boldsymbol {f} _ {j} - \delta_ {i j} \right| \\ \leq \left| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {j} - \delta_ {i j} \right| + \left| \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \right| \left| \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {j} \right| \\ \leq \frac {\kappa_ {p}}{\sqrt {p}} + \frac {\kappa_ {p} ^ {2}}{p} \leq \frac {c \kappa_ {p}}{\sqrt {p}} \\ \end{array}
$$

for some positive constant  $c$ . finally, by substituting the bounds (68) [with  $b_{p}$  there replaced by  $b_{p}(s)$ ] and (106) into (105), we reach the target inequality in (98).

# c. proof of theorem 2

to go from lemma 3 to theorem 2, we just need to remove the following two restrictions in the assumptions of lemma 3: (1)  $\sigma(x)$  is compactly supported on  $[- \tau_p, \tau_p]$  for some  $\tau_p = \mathrm{polylog}p$ ; and (2)  $\varphi(x;s)$  and its derivatives are bounded [see (97)]. the main ingredient of our proof is to show, via a standard truncation technique, that the central limit theorem characterization still holds even if we relax these two assumptions.

let  $\varphi(x; s)$  be a test function satisfying (66). we can construct a smoothly truncated version of this function via

$$
\widehat {\varphi} _ {p} (x; s) \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \varphi (x; s) \omega_ {t _ {p}, 1} (x),
$$

where  $\omega_{t_p,1}(x)$  is the smooth window function defined in (119) in appendix a and

$$
t _ {p} = \left(\| \boldsymbol {f} \| + 1\right) \left(\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| / \sqrt {p}\right) \sqrt {c _ {t} \log p} \tag {107}
$$

for some positive constant  $c_t$ . the threshold  $t_p$  in (107) is chosen strategically. with this choice, we can show

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left. \mathbb {e} \left| \varphi \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| \right. \tag {108} \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \left(1 + \left(\| \beta \| / \sqrt {p}\right) ^ {k}\right) \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p}. \\ \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left. \mathbb {e} \left| \varphi \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {b} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {b} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| \right. \tag {109} \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \left(1 + \left(\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| / \sqrt {p}\right) ^ {k}\right) \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p}. \\ \end{array}
$$

the detailed proof of (108) and (109) are provided in appendix b together, (108) and (109) show that replacing the original test function  $\varphi(x;s)$  with its smoothly truncated approximation  $\widehat{\varphi}_p(x;s)$  only incurs a small price of  $\mathcal{o}(\mathrm{polylog}p / \sqrt{p})$ .

next, we consider the activation function  $\sigma(x)$ . using the smooth window function in (119) again, we can build a truncated approximation

$$
\widehat {\sigma} _ {p} (x) \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \sigma (x) \omega_ {\tau_ {p}, 1} (x), \quad \text {w h e r e} \tau_ {p} = \sqrt {2 c _ {\tau} \log p} \tag {110}
$$

for some positive constant  $c_{\tau}$ . it is easy to verify that  $\widehat{\sigma}_p(x)$  satisfies all the assumptions stated in lemma 3 concerning the activation functions. with this truncated activation function, define

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {a}} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \widehat {\sigma} _ {p} (\boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f}) \quad \text {a n d} \quad \widehat {\boldsymbol {b}} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \mu_ {1, p} \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} + \mu_ {2, p} \boldsymbol {z} \tag {111}
$$

as the counterparts of  $\mathbf{a}$  and  $\mathbf{b}$  in (62). here,  $\mu_{1,p}, \mu_{2,p}$  are the constants defined in (69). our goal is to show that

$\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}} \boldsymbol{a}^{\mathsf{t}} \boldsymbol{\beta} \approx \frac{1}{\sqrt{p}} \widehat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\mathsf{t}} \boldsymbol{\beta}$  and  $\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}} \boldsymbol{b}^{\mathsf{t}} \boldsymbol{\beta} \approx \frac{1}{\sqrt{p}} \widehat{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\mathsf{t}} \boldsymbol{\beta}$ . specifically, we can get (details are relegated to appendix b)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left| \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \widehat {\boldsymbol {a}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \left[ 1 + \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \|\right) ^ {2 k + 1} \right] \text {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p}. \tag {112} \\ \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left| \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {b} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \widehat {\boldsymbol {b}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \left[ 1 + \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \|\right) ^ {2 k + 1} \right] \frac {\operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}}. \tag {113} \\ \end{array}
$$

given the inequalities in (108), (109), (112) and (113), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} \varphi \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \beta ; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \mathbb {e} \varphi \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {b} ^ {\top} \beta ; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \left[ 1 + \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \|\right) ^ {2 k + 1} \right] \frac {\operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}} \\ + \left| \mathbb {e} \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \widehat {\boldsymbol {a}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \mathbb {e} \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \widehat {\boldsymbol {b}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right|. \\ \end{array}
$$

we can use lemma 3 to bound the second term on the righthand side, since its test function  $\widehat{\varphi}_p(x;s)$  and the activation function  $\widehat{\sigma}_p(x)$  satisfy the assumptions stated in that lemma. using (98) and the property that  $|\mu_2 - \mu_{2,p}|\leq \mathrm{polylog}p / \sqrt{p}$ , we reach the main result (67) of the theorem.

# d. extension to piecewise smooth test functions

in what follows, we generalize theorem 2 to test functions that are only piecewise differentiable. this auxiliary result will be needed in our proof of proposition 1 for the case where the "output function"  $\theta_{\mathrm{out}}(y)$  in the generalization error (4) lacks smoothness [e.g.,  $\theta_{\mathrm{out}}(y) = \mathrm{sign}(y)]$

proposition 3. consider the same assumptions of theorem 2 with “ $\varphi_p(x;s)$  is differentiable with respect to  $x$ ” replaced by “ $\varphi_p(x;s)$  differentiable with respect to  $x$  except at a finite number of points  $\{x_1,x_2,\ldots ,x_l\}$ ". additionally, we also assume that

1) the upper bound  $\kappa_{p} \leq \mathrm{polylog} p$  in (64).  
2)  $\| \beta \|_{\infty}\leq \mathrm{polylog}p$  
3) let  $\nu^2 = \beta^\top \sigma \beta / p$ , where  $\sigma$  is the covariance matrix in (12). then  $\nu^2 \geq c > 0$  for some constant  $c$ .

it then holds that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {b} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| _ {[ \mathbb {e} r ^ {4} (z) ] ^ {1 / 4} \text {p o l y l o g} p.} (114) \\ \leq \frac {\left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{p ^ {1 / 8}}, (114) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $z\sim \mathcal{n}(0,1)$

remark 6. it is possible to improve the convergence rate on the right-hand side of (114) from  $\mathcal{o}(p^{-1/8} \mathrm{polylog} p)$  to  $\mathcal{o}(p^{-1/4} \mathrm{polylog} p)$ , by requiring higher moments of  $b_p(z)$  to be bounded. we do not pursue this optimization as the current form is sufficient for our proof of proposition 1.

proof. see appendix c

# iv. conclusion and final remarks

in this paper, we have proved the asymptotic equivalence of a nonlinear random feature model and a surrogate linear gaussian models in terms of their training and generalization errors. as a consequence of this universality theorem, the learning performance of high-dimensional random feature models can be precisely characterized by studying their linear gaussian counterparts, which are much more amenable to theoretical analysis. our proof, which builds on the classical lindeberg approach, makes several technical assumptions on the loss function, the nonlinear activation function, and the feature matrix. we close the paper by discussing how some of these assumptions can be further relaxed.

non-differentiable loss functions. in assumption (a.4), we require the loss function  $\ell(x; y)$  to have bounded third partial derivatives with respect to  $x$ . many loss functions used in practice are not differentiable everywhere. a notable example is the hinge loss for binary classification, where  $\ell(x; y) = \ell_{\text{hinge}}(yx)$  with  $\ell_{\text{hinge}}(x) = \max(0, 1 - x)$ . one way to extend our current analysis to such non-differentiable functions is to consider a smoothed approximation via convolution. in the case of the hinge loss, let

$$
\ell_ {\text {h i n g e}, \delta} (x) = \int_ {\mathbb {r}} \ell_ {\text {h i n g e}} (x - z) \zeta_ {\delta} (z) d z,
$$

where  $\zeta_{\delta}(z)$  is a scaled mollifier defined in (118). it is easy to verify that, for every  $\delta > 0$ ,  $\ell_{\mathrm{hinge},\delta}(x)$  is convex,  $\| \ell_{\mathrm{hinge},\delta}^{\prime \prime \prime}(x)\|_{\infty}\leq c / \delta^{3}$ , and

$$
\left\| \ell_ {\text {h i n g e}} (x) - \ell_ {\text {h i n g e}, \delta} (x) \right\| _ {\infty} \leq c \delta , \tag {115}
$$

for some  $c > 0$ . recall that  $\phi_a$  and  $\phi_b$  denote the training errors [i.e., the minimum value of the optimization problem in (11)] of the nonlinear feature model and the linear gaussian model, respectively. we now use  $\phi_a^\delta$  and  $\phi_b^\delta$  to denote the corresponding quantities if we replace the hinge loss in (11) by its smooth version  $\ell_{\mathrm{hinge},\delta}(x)$ . it follows from (115) that  $\left|\frac{1}{p}\phi_a - \frac{1}{p}\phi_a^\delta\right| \leq c(n/p)\delta$  and  $\left|\frac{1}{p}\phi_b - \frac{1}{p}\phi_b^\delta\right| \leq c(n/p)\delta$ . the left-hand side of (18) can now be bounded as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} \varphi \left(\frac {1}{p} \phi_ {\boldsymbol {a}}\right) - \mathbb {e} \varphi \left(\frac {1}{p} \phi_ {\boldsymbol {b}}\right) \right| \tag {116} \\ \leq 2 c (n / p) \| \varphi^ {\prime} \| _ {\infty} \delta + \left| \mathbb {e} \varphi \left(\frac {1}{p} \phi_ {\boldsymbol {a}} ^ {\delta}\right) - \mathbb {e} \varphi \left(\frac {1}{p} \phi_ {\boldsymbol {b}} ^ {\delta}\right) \right|. \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $\ell_{\mathrm{hinge},\delta}(x)$  satisfies assumption (a.4), we can apply our current analysis to bound the second term on the right-hand side of (116). we have the freedom in choosing the parameter  $\delta$ . clearly,  $\delta$  must go to 0 as  $p\to \infty$ , but it cannot be too small. this is because  $\| \ell_{\mathrm{hinge},\delta}^{\prime \prime \prime}(x)\|_{\infty}\leq c / \delta^{3}$ , and this bound on the third derivative is hidden in our estimates in lemma 16, lemma 21 and lemma 22. by choosing an optimal rate of decay for  $\delta$ , we can show that the left-hand side of (116) tends to 0 as  $p$  grows, albeit with a slower convergence rate than that given in (18). note that similar smoothing techniques can also be used to extend our analysis to non-differentiable activation functions and regularizers.

more general activation functions. as a main limitation of our current work, we have assumed that the activation function  $\sigma (x)$  is odd. under this assumption, the regression

vectors  $\{\pmb{a}_t\}$  in (6) and  $\{\pmb{b}_t\}$  in (7) have zero mean and this simplifies our proof. as shown in figure 1(a), the universality phenomenon holds under more general activation functions, including e.g.,  $\sigma(x) = \max(x,0)$ . one possible way to extend our work to such cases is the following. let  $\widetilde{\pmb{a}}_t = \pmb{a}_t - \mu_0\mathbf{1}$  and  $\widetilde{\pmb{b}}_t = \pmb{b}_t - \mu_0\mathbf{1}$ , where  $\mu_0$  is the constant in (8). then  $\widetilde{\pmb{a}}_t$  and  $\widetilde{\pmb{b}}_t$  have (approximately) zero mean. we also rewrite the optimization problem in (11) as an equivalent two stage process:  $\phi_a(\tau_1,\tau_2) = \min_{c\in \mathbb{r}}\phi_a(c,\tau_1,\tau_2)$ , where

$$
\begin{array}{l} \phi_ {\boldsymbol {a}} (c, \tau_ {1}, \tau_ {2}) \\ = \inf  _ {\mathbf {1} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {w} / \sqrt {p} = c} \left\{\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {n} \ell \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {t} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {w} + \mu_ {0} c; y _ {t}\right) + \right. \\ \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {p} h (w _ {j}) + \tau_ {1} (\boldsymbol {w} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\sigma} \boldsymbol {w}) + \tau_ {2} (\sqrt {p} \mu_ {1} \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {w}) \}. \\ \end{array}
$$

we can define  $\phi_b(c, \tau_1, \tau_2)$  in a similar way. since  $\mathbb{e}[\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_t] \approx \mathbf{0}$  and  $\mathbb{e}[\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}_t] = \mathbf{0}$ , it is not difficult to extend our current analysis to show that  $\phi_a(c, \tau_1, \tau_2) / p \approx \phi_b(c, \tau_1, \tau_2) / p$ . the remaining challenge is to show that this approximate equivalence holds uniformly over  $c$ , potentially by exploiting the convexity of the functions  $\phi_a(c, \tau_1, \tau_2)$  and  $\phi_a(c, \tau_1, \tau_2)$  with respect to  $c$ .

deterministic feature matrices. yet another limitation of our work is that we have only considered cases where the columns of the feature matrix  $\pmb{f}$  are independent gaussian vectors. in fact, most of our technical results (such as those stated in section ii-c) have been obtained when we condition on a fixed  $\pmb{f}$  that satisfies (64) and (65). the only place where we use the randomness of  $\pmb{f}$  is in lemma 23 and proposition 2, where we show that the  $\ell_{\infty}$ -norm of the optimal weight vector  $\pmb{w}_k^*$  is bounded by  $\text{polylog } p$  with high probability. this bound on the  $\ell_{\infty}$ -norm is needed in the central limit theorem stated in theorem 2. [see, in particular, (67).] thus, an important open problem is to check if  $\| \pmb{w}_k^* \|_{\infty} \leq \text{polylog } p$  with high probability for deterministic feature matrices that satisfy (64) and (65).

# appendix

# a. smoothing and truncation

in our proofs, we often need to apply smoothing and truncation to certain functions. this appendix collects the background and auxiliary results associated with such operations. first, we recall the construction of a standard mollifier

$$
\zeta (x) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} c   e ^ {- 1 / (1 - x ^ {2})}, & \quad \text {i f} | x | <   1 \\ 0, & \quad \text {i f} | x | \geq 1, \end{array} \right.
$$

where the constant  $c$  ensures that  $\int_{\mathbb{r}} \zeta(x) dx = 1$ . by construction,  $\zeta(x)$  is compactly supported and nonnegative. it is also easy to show that  $\zeta(x)$  is infinitely differentiable and that

$$
\max  \left\{\| \zeta (x) \| _ {\infty}, \| \zeta^ {\prime} (x) \| _ {\infty}, \| \zeta^ {\prime \prime} (x) \| _ {\infty} \right\} \leq c \tag {117}
$$

for some numerical constant  $c$ . for each  $\delta > 0$ , we can rescale the mollifier as

$$
\zeta_ {\delta} (x) = \delta^ {- 1} \zeta (x / \delta) \tag {118}
$$

so that the resulting function is supported on  $[- \delta, \delta]$ . for any piecewise-smooth function  $h(x)$ , we can obtain a smooth approximation by convolving it with a mollifier, i.e.,

$$
h _ {\delta} (x) \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} (h * \zeta_ {\delta}) (x) = \int_ {\mathbb {r}} \zeta_ {\delta} (x - y) h (y) d y.
$$

a special case, frequently used in our proofs, is when  $h(x)$  is the indicator function defined on certain intervals. in particular, for  $t > 0, \delta > 0$ , we define

$$
\omega_ {t, \delta} (x) = \left(\mathbb {1} _ {[ - t - \delta / 2, t + \delta / 2 ]} * \zeta_ {\delta / 2}\right) (x) \tag {119}
$$

as a smooth "window function". it is easy to check that  $\omega_{t,\delta}(x) = 1$  for  $|x|\leq t$ ,  $\omega_{t,\delta}(x) = 0$  for  $|x|\geq t + \delta$ , and  $0\leq \omega_{t,\delta}(x)\leq 1$  for  $x$  in the smooth "transition bands". moreover, it follows from (117) that  $\| \omega_{t,\delta}'(x)\|_{\infty}\leq c / \delta$ .

lemma 4. let  $h(x)$  be a function that is differentiable everywhere except at a finite number of points  $\{x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_l\}$ . if there is a function  $b(x)$  such that

$$
\left| h ^ {\prime} (x) \right| \leq b (x), f o r x \notin \{x _ {1}, x _ {2}, \dots , x _ {k} \} a n d | h (x) | \leq b (x) \tag {120}
$$

then for every  $\delta > 0$ ,

$$
\left| h (x) - h _ {\delta} (x) \right| \leq b _ {\delta} (x) \delta + 2 b _ {\delta} (x) \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {l} \omega_ {2 \delta , \delta} \left(x - x _ {i}\right), \tag {121}
$$

where  $b_{\delta}(x)\stackrel {def}{=}\sup_{|c|\leq \delta}b(x + c)$  and  $\omega_{2\delta ,\delta}(\cdot)$  is a smoothed window function as defined in (119). moreover,

$$
\left| h _ {\delta} (x) \right| \leq b _ {\delta} (x) \quad a n d \quad \left| h _ {\delta} ^ {\prime} (x) \right| \leq \frac {c b _ {\delta} (x)}{\delta} \tag {122}
$$

for some numerical constant  $c$ .

proof. let  $\mathcal{d} = \cup_{1\leq i\leq l}[x_i - 2\delta ,x_i + 2\delta ]$  . for any  $x\notin \mathcal{d}$  , the function  $h(x)$  is differentiable on the interval  $[x - \delta ,x + \delta ]$  for such  $x$  , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| h (x) - h _ {\delta} (x) \right| \stackrel {(a)} {=} \left| \int_ {| y - x | \leq \delta} [ h (x) - h (y) ] \zeta_ {\delta} (x - y) d y \right| \\ \leq \int_ {| y - x | \leq \delta} | h (x) - h (y) | \zeta_ {\delta} (x - y) d y \\ \stackrel {(b)} {\leq} b _ {\delta} (x) \delta , \tag {123} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $(a)$  uses the property that  $\int_{\mathbb{r}}\zeta \delta (x - y)dy = 1$ , and  $(b)$  is due to the intermediate value theorem and (120). for any  $x\in \mathcal{d}$ , we directly use the bound on  $h(x)$  to get

$$
\left| h (x) - h _ {\delta} (x) \right| \leq \int_ {| y - x | \leq \delta} \left| h (x) - h (y) \right| \zeta_ {\delta} (x - y) d y \leq 2 b _ {\delta} (x). \tag {124}
$$

combining (123) and (124) gives us

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| h (x) - h _ {\delta} (x) \right| = \left| h (x) - h _ {\delta} (x) \right| \mathbb {1} _ {\mathcal {d} ^ {c}} (x) + \left| h (x) - h _ {\delta} (x) \right| \mathbb {1} _ {\mathcal {d}} (x) \\ \leq b _ {\delta} (x) \delta + 2 b _ {\delta} (x) \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {l} \mathbb {1} _ {[ x _ {i} - 2 \delta , x _ {i} + 2 \delta ]} (x). \\ \end{array}
$$

the desired inequality in (121) then follows from the simple observation that  $\mathbb{1}_{[x_i - 2\delta ,x_i + 2\delta ]}(x)\leq \omega_{2\delta ,\delta}(x - x_i)$ , which can be easily verified from the definition in (119).

the first inequality in (122) is obvious. to get the second inequality, we have

$$
h _ {\delta} ^ {\prime} (x) = \int_ {| y - x | \leq \delta} h (y) \frac {1}{\delta^ {2}} \zeta^ {\prime} \left(\frac {x - y}{\delta}\right) d y \leq \frac {b _ {\delta} (x)}{\delta} \| \zeta^ {\prime} (x) \| _ {\infty},
$$

and this completes the proof.

![](images/84b2d17f914a29a36392f2b412b93dacd1dba9c275300bea6356c84b430570d4.jpg)

# b. auxiliary results for the proof of theorem 2

# 1. proof of (108) and (109).

let  $\mathcal{b}$  be the event that  $\left\{\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\mathrm{t}}\boldsymbol {\beta}\right|\leq t_p\right\}$ . applying lemma 8 in appendix e2, we get  $\mathbb{p}(\mathcal{b})\geq 1 - 2p^{-c_t / c}$ , where  $c > 0$  is some fixed numerical constant. thus, by using a sufficiently large  $c_t$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {p} (\mathcal {b}) \geq 1 - 2 / p. \tag {125}
$$

the standard trick in a truncation method is to introduce two indicator functions defined on  $\mathcal{b}$  and  $\mathcal{b}^c$ , respectively. since

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \varphi \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \beta ; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \beta ; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| 1 _ {\mathcal {b}} \equiv 0, \\ \mathbb {e} \left| \varphi \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| \\ = \mathbb {e} \left[ \left| \varphi \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| \mathbb {1} _ {\mathcal {b}} ^ {c} \right] \\ \leq 2 \left[ \mathbb {e} \varphi^ {2} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \left[ 1 - \mathbb {p} (\mathcal {b}) \right] ^ {1 / 2}, \tag {126} \\ \end{array}
$$

where to reach (126) we have used hölder's inequality and the fact that  $|\varphi(x;s)| \geq |\widehat{\varphi}_p(x;s)|$ . to bound the first term on the right-hand side of (126), we can use (66) and get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \varphi^ {2} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \leq \mathbb {e} b ^ {2} (\boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}) \left(1 + \left| \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta} \right| ^ {k}\right) ^ {2} \\ \leq 2 \sqrt {2} \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (\boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \left[ 1 + \mathbb {e} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}\right) ^ {4 k} \right] ^ {1 / 2} \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \left(1 + \left(\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| / \sqrt {p}\right) ^ {2 k}\right) \text {p o l y} \log p, \tag {127} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality is obtained by using the moment estimate (157) in lemma 8. substituting (125) and (127) into (126), we can get (108). the steps leading to (109) are completely analogous to what we did to reach (108), so we omit the details here.

# 2. proof of (112) and (113).

first, we prove (112). let

$$
\mathcal {d} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \left\{\max  _ {i \leq p} \left| \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \right| \leq \tau_ {p} \right\}. \tag {128}
$$

by construction,  $\pmb{a} = \widehat{\pmb{a}}$  when the event  $\mathcal{d}$  holds. next, we show that  $\mathcal{d}$  is indeed a high-probability event. recall that  $\pmb{g}^{\top}\pmb{f}_{i}\stackrel {\mathrm{law}}{=}\| \pmb{f}_{i}\| z$  for  $z\sim \mathcal{n}(0,1)$ . moreover, the condition in (64) implies that  $\max_i\| f_i\| ^2\leq c$  for some fixed constant  $c$ . a standard gaussian tail bound  $\mathbb{p}(|z|\geq t)\leq 2e^{-t^2 /2}$  then gives us

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {p} \left(\mathcal {d} ^ {c}\right) \leq \sum_ {i \leq p} \mathbb {p} \left(| z | \geq \frac {\tau_ {p}}{\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \|}\right) \tag {129} \\ \leq 2 p e ^ {- \tau_ {p} ^ {2} / (2 c)} \leq 2 p ^ {- (c _ {\tau} / c - 1)} \leq 2 / p \\ \end{array}
$$

for all sufficiently large  $c_{\tau}$ . [without loss of generality, we should also assume that  $c_{\tau} \geq 2$ , as this is needed in the proof of an auxiliary result in appendix d.] on the other hand, by

the construction of  $\omega_{t_p,1}(x)$  and the assumption in (66), we can easily verify that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \max  \left\{\| \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} (x; s) \| _ {\infty}, \| \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} ^ {\prime} (x; s) \| _ {\infty} \right\} \\ \leq \widehat {b} _ {p} (s) \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \left[ 1 + \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \| \beta \|\right) ^ {2 k} \right] b _ {p} (s) \text {p o l y l o g} p, \tag {130} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $k$  is the constant in (66). then using the boundedness of  $\widehat{\varphi}_p^\prime (x;s)$  given in (130) and defining  $\mathbb{1}_{\mathcal{d}^c}$  as the indicator function supported on  $\mathcal{d}^c$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left| \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \widehat {\boldsymbol {a}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right| \\ \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ \widehat {b} _ {p} (\boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}) \left| \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta} - \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \widehat {\boldsymbol {a}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta} \right| \mathbb {1} _ {\mathcal {d} ^ {c}} \right] \\ \stackrel {(a)} {\leq} 8 ^ {1 / 4} \left[ \mathbb {e} \widehat {b} _ {p} ^ {4} (\boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\xi}) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \left(\left[ \mathbb {e} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta}\right) ^ {4} \right] ^ {1 / 4} \right. \\ \left. + \left[ \mathbb {e} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \widehat {\boldsymbol {a}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}\right) ^ {4} \right] ^ {1 / 4}\right) \sqrt {\mathbb {p} (\mathcal {d} ^ {c})} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \stackrel {(b)} {\leq} \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \left[ 1 + \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \|\right) ^ {2 k} \right] \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \|\right) \text {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p} \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \left[ 1 + \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \| \beta \|\right) ^ {2 k + 1} \right] \text {p o l y l o g} p / \sqrt {p}, \tag {131} \\ \end{array}
$$

which is (112). here, (a) is based on a generalized hölder's inequality:  $\mathbb{e}|xyz| \leq (\mathbb{e}x^4\mathbb{e}y^4)^{1/4}(\mathbb{e}z^2)^{1/2}$ . to reach (b), we use (130) and the moment bound (157) in lemma 8.

next we prove (113). it follows from the definition in (111) that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {b} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta} - \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta} \right| \leq \left| \mu_ {1} - \mu_ {1, p} \right| \left| \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {\beta} \right| \\ + \left| \mu_ {2} - \mu_ {2, p} \right| \left| \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} z ^ {\mathsf {t}} \beta \right| \\ \leq \frac {\operatorname {p o l y} \log p}{\sqrt {p}} \big (\left| \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {\beta} \right| + \left| \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta} \right| \big), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality uses the estimate given in lemma 6 in appendix d. we now have

$$
\mathbb {e} \left| \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {b} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) - \widehat {\varphi} _ {p} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \widehat {\boldsymbol {b}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}; \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\xi}\right) \right|
$$

$$
\leq \mathbb {e} \left[ \widehat {b} _ {p} (\boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\xi}) \left(\left| \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {\beta} \right| + \left| \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\beta} \right|\right) \right] \frac {\text {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \left[ 1 + \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \|\right) ^ {2 k} \right] \left(\sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} \boldsymbol {\beta}\right) ^ {2}} \right. \\ \left. + \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta}\right) ^ {2}}\right) \frac {\operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \left[ 1 + \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \|\right) ^ {2 k + 1} \right] \frac {\operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}}, \tag {132}
$$

which is (113).

# c. proof of proposition 3

for any  $\delta_p\in (0,1)$  , let

$$
\varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} (x; s) = \int \varphi_ {p} (y; s) \zeta_ {\delta_ {p}} (x - y) d y
$$

be a smoothed version of the test function, where  $\zeta_{\delta_p}(x)$  is the mollifier introduced in appendix a. the main idea of the proof is choosing a diminishing sequence of  $\delta_p$  so that the left-hand side of (114) is well-approximated by a similar term involving the smooth function  $\varphi_{\delta_p}(x;s)$ . to shorten notation, in what follows, we abbreviate  $\varphi_p\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\boldsymbol{a}^\top \boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{g}^\top \boldsymbol{\xi}\right)$  and  $\varphi_{\delta_p}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\boldsymbol{a}^\top \boldsymbol{\beta};\boldsymbol{g}^\top \boldsymbol{\xi}\right)$  to  $\varphi (\boldsymbol {a})$  and  $\varphi_{\delta_p}(\boldsymbol {a})$ , respectively. the

meaning of the notation  $\varphi (\pmb {b})$  and  $\varphi_{\delta_p}(\pmb {b})$  should also be clear. since

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} \varphi (\boldsymbol {a}) - \mathbb {e} \varphi (\boldsymbol {b}) \right| \leq \left| \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} (\boldsymbol {a}) - \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} (\boldsymbol {b}) \right| \\ + \mathbb {e} | \varphi (\boldsymbol {a}) - \varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} (\boldsymbol {a}) | + \mathbb {e} | \varphi (\boldsymbol {b}) - \varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} (\boldsymbol {b}) |, \tag {133} \\ \end{array}
$$

we just need to bound the three terms on the right-hand side.

the first term can be controlled by theorem 2, as  $\varphi_{\delta_p}(x;s)$  is differentiable. by assumption,  $|\varphi (x;s)|\leq b_p(s)(1 + |x|^k)$  for some  $k\geq 1$ . using the simple bound (122) in lemma 4 (see appendix a), we can check that, for any  $\delta_p < 1$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \max  \left\{\left| \varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} (x, s) \right|, \left| \varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} ^ {\prime} (x, s) \right| \right\} \\ \leq \frac {c b _ {p} (s) [ 1 + (| x | + \delta_ {p}) ^ {k} ]}{\delta_ {p}} \leq \frac {c ^ {\prime} b _ {p} (s) [ 1 + | x | ^ {k} ]}{\delta_ {p}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $c$  is some numerical constant and  $c' = (2^{k - 1} + 1)c$ . theorem 2 then gives us

$$
\left| \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} (\boldsymbol {a}) - \mathbb {e} \varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} (\boldsymbol {b}) \right| \leq \frac {\left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\delta_ {p} \sqrt {p}}, \tag {134}
$$

where we have simplified the term  $p(\boldsymbol{\beta}, \kappa_p)$  in (67) by using the additional assumption that  $\kappa_p \leq \mathrm{polylog} p$  and  $\| \boldsymbol{\beta} \|_{\infty} \leq \mathrm{polylog} p$ .

to control the second term on the right-hand side of (133), we apply lemma 4 again. using a shorthand notation  $\widehat{b}_p(\boldsymbol{a}) = c'b_p(s)(1 + \left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\boldsymbol{a}^\top \boldsymbol{\beta}\right|^k)$ , we have, from (121),

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left| \varphi (\boldsymbol {a}) - \varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} (\boldsymbol {a}) \right| \\ \leq \delta_ {p} \mathbb {e} \widehat {b} _ {p} (\boldsymbol {a}) + 2 \sum_ {i \leq l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \widehat {b} _ {p} (\boldsymbol {a}) \omega_ {2 \delta_ {p}, \delta_ {p}} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta} - x _ {i}\right) \right] \\ \leq \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \hat {b} _ {p} ^ {2} (\boldsymbol {a})} \left[ \delta_ {p} + 2 \sum_ {i \leq l} \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \omega_ {2 \delta_ {p} , \delta_ {p}} ^ {2} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta} - x _ {i}\right)} \right] \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p \\ \times \left[ \delta_ {p} + 2 \sum_ {i \leq l} \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \omega_ {2 \delta_ {p} , \delta_ {p}} ^ {2} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta} - x _ {i}\right)} \right], \tag {135} \\ \end{array}
$$

where in reaching the last step we have used the moment bound obtained in (127). the same reasoning also yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left| \varphi (\boldsymbol {b}) - \varphi_ {\delta_ {p}} (\boldsymbol {b}) \right| \\ \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \text {p o l y l o g} p \tag {136} \\ \times \left[ \delta_ {p} + 2 \sum_ {i \leq l} \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \omega_ {2 \delta_ {p} , \delta_ {p}} ^ {2} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \pmb {b} ^ {\top} \pmb {\beta} - x _ {i}\right)} \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

note that  $\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}$  is a gaussian random variable with zero mean and variance  $\nu^{2}$ . (recall the definition of  $\nu^{2}$  in the statement of the proposition.) as the function  $\omega_{2\delta_p,\delta_p}^2 (x - x_i)\leq 1$  with a compact support of width  $6\delta_{p}$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {e} \omega_ {2 \delta_ {p}, \delta_ {p}} ^ {2} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {b} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta} - x _ {i}\right) \leq \frac {6 \delta_ {p}}{\sqrt {2 \pi \nu^ {2}}} \leq c \delta_ {p}, \tag {137}
$$

where the second inequality is by the assumption that  $\nu^2 \geq c > 0$  for some fixed  $c$ . this bound can also be leveraged to control  $\mathbb{e}\omega_{2\delta_p,\delta_p}^2 (\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\boldsymbol{a}^\top \boldsymbol {\beta} - x_i)$ . indeed,  $\omega_{2\delta_p,\delta_p}^2 (x - x_i)$  is a

smooth and bounded test function whose derivative is bounded by  $c / \delta_p$ . by theorem 2,

$$
\left| \mathbb {e} \omega_ {2 \delta_ {p}, \delta_ {p}} ^ {2} (\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \pmb {a} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \pmb {\beta} - x _ {i}) - \mathbb {e} \omega_ {2 \delta_ {p}, \delta_ {p}} ^ {2} (\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \pmb {b} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \pmb {\beta} - x _ {i}) \right| \leq \frac {\mathrm {p o l y l o g} p}{\delta_ {p} \sqrt {p}},
$$

and thus

$$
\mathbb {e} \omega_ {2 \delta_ {p}, \delta_ {p}} ^ {2} \left(\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\beta} - x _ {i}\right) \leq \left[ \delta_ {p} + \frac {1}{\delta_ {p} \sqrt {p}} \right] \text {p o l y l o g} p. \tag {138}
$$

substituting (138), (137), (135), (136), (134) into (133), and after some simplifications, we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} \varphi (\boldsymbol {a}) - \mathbb {e} \varphi (\boldsymbol {b}) \right| \leq \left[ \mathbb {e} b _ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 4} \text {p o l y l o g} p \\ \times \left[ \delta_ {p} + (\delta_ {p} \sqrt {p}) ^ {- 1} + \sqrt {\delta_ {p} + (\delta_ {p} \sqrt {p}) ^ {- 1}} \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

the convergence rate of the right-hand side can be optimized by setting  $\delta_p = p^{-1/4}$ . this then leads to the claim in (114).

# d. asymptotic equivalence of the covariance matrices

consider a sequence of activation functions  $\{\sigma_p(x)\}$  such that, for every  $p$ ,  $\sigma_p(x)$  is an odd function and

$$
\max  \left\{\| \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} (x) \| _ {\infty}, \| \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime \prime} (x) \| _ {\infty}, \| \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime \prime \prime} (x) \| _ {\infty} \right\} \leq \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p.
$$

given a set of feature vectors  $\pmb{f} = [f_{1}, f_{2}, \dots, f_{p}] \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times p}$ , we define

$$
\boldsymbol {a} \stackrel {{\text {d e f}}} {{=}} \sigma (\boldsymbol {f} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {g}) \quad \text {a n d} \quad \boldsymbol {b} = \mu_ {1, p} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {g} + \mu_ {2, p} \boldsymbol {z},
$$

where  $\pmb{g} \sim \mathcal{n}(0, \pmb{i}_d)$  and  $\pmb{z} \sim \mathcal{n}(0, \pmb{i}_p)$  are two independent gaussian vectors, and  $\mu_{1,p} = \mathbb{e}[z\sigma_p(z)]$ ,  $\mu_{2,p} = \sqrt{\mathbb{e}\sigma_p^2(z) - \mu_{1,p}^2}$ , with  $z \sim \mathcal{n}(0, 1)$ , are two constants. the primary goal of this appendix is to quantify the difference between the covariance matrices

$$
\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {a} = \mathbb {e} \boldsymbol {a} \boldsymbol {a} ^ {\top} \quad \text {a n d} \quad \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {b} = \mathbb {e} \boldsymbol {b} \boldsymbol {b} ^ {\top} = \mu_ {1, p} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} + \mu_ {2, p} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} _ {p}.
$$

we start by noting that  $\mu_{1,p} = \mathbb{e}\sigma_p'(z)$  and thus

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} (\boldsymbol {g} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {i}) - \mu_ {1, p} \right| \leq \mathbb {e} \left| \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} (\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \| z) - \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} (z) \right| \\ \leq \left\| \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime \prime} (x) \right\| _ {\infty} (\mathbb {e} | z |) \left\| f _ {i} \right\| - 1 \\ \leq \left(\operatorname {p o l y} \log p\right) \left| \left\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \right\| ^ {2} - 1 \right|. \tag {139} \\ \end{array}
$$

lemma 5. suppose that the feature vectors satisfy (64) with some  $\kappa_{p}$ . we have

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {a} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {b} \right\| \leq \frac {\left(1 + \kappa_ {p} ^ {3} + \| \boldsymbol {f} \| ^ {4}\right) \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}}. \tag {140}
$$

proof. the  $(i,j)$ th entry of  $\pmb{\sigma}_{a}$  is  $\mathbb{e}[\sigma_p(\pmb{g}^\top \pmb{f}_i)\sigma_p(\pmb{g}^\top \pmb{f}_j)]$ . since  $(\pmb{g}^\top \pmb{f}_i,\pmb{g}^\top \pmb{f}_j)$  are jointly gaussian, we can rewrite their joint distribution as that of  $(z_{i},\rho_{ij}z_{i} + \sqrt{1 - \rho_{ij}\rho_{ji}} z_{j})$ , where  $z_{i}\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\| \pmb{f}_{i}\|^{2}),z_{j}\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\| \pmb{f}_{j}\|^{2})$  are two independent gaussian random variables and  $\rho_{ij}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\pmb{f}_i^\top \pmb{f}_j / \| \pmb{f}_i\|^2$ . note that the definition of  $\rho_{ij}$  is not symmetric:  $\rho_{ij}\neq \rho_{ji}$  unless

$\| \pmb{f}_i\| = \| \pmb{f}_j\|$  .with this new representation, we have, for  $i\neq j$

$$
\pmb {\sigma} _ {a} (i, j)
$$

$$
= \mathbb {e} \left[ \sigma_ {p} \left(z _ {i}\right) \sigma_ {p} \left(\rho_ {i j} z _ {i} + \sqrt {1 - \rho_ {i j} \rho_ {j i}} z _ {j}\right) \right]
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \stackrel {(a)} {=} \mathbb {e} \left[ \sigma_ {p} \left(z _ {i}\right) \sigma_ {p} \left(\sqrt {1 - \rho_ {i j} \rho_ {j i}} z _ {j}\right) \right] \\ + \rho_ {i j} \mathbb {e} \left[ \sigma_ {p} \left(z _ {i}\right) z _ {i} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} \left(\sqrt {1 - \rho_ {i j} \rho_ {j i}} z _ {j}\right) \right] \\ + \frac {1}{2} \rho_ {i j} ^ {2} \mathbb {e} \left[ \sigma_ {p} \left(z _ {i}\right) z _ {i} ^ {2} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime \prime} \left(\sqrt {1 - \rho_ {i j} \rho_ {j i}} z _ {j}\right) \right] \\ + \frac {1}{6} \rho_ {i j} ^ {3} \mathbb {e} \left[ \sigma_ {p} \left(z _ {i}\right) z _ {i} ^ {3} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime \prime \prime} \left(\theta_ {i j}\right) \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \stackrel {(b)} {=} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {f} _ {j}\right) \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} \left(z _ {i}\right) \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} \left(\sqrt {1 - \rho_ {i j} \rho_ {j i}} z _ {j}\right) \\ + \frac {1}{6} \rho_ {i j} ^ {3} \mathbb {e} [ \sigma_ {p} (z _ {i}) z _ {i} ^ {3} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime \prime \prime} (\theta_ {i j}) ] \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\stackrel {(c)} {=} \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {f} _ {j}\right) \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} \left(z _ {i}\right) \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} \left(z _ {j}\right) + r _ {i j}. \tag {141}
$$

here, (a) comes from taylor's series expansion, with  $\theta_{ij}$  being some point between  $\sqrt{1 - \rho_{ij} \rho_{ji}} z_j$  and  $\rho_{ij} z_i + \sqrt{1 - \rho_{ij} \rho_{ji}} z_j$ . to reach (b), we have used the independence between  $z_i$  and  $z_j$ , and the following identities:  $\mathbb{e} \sigma_p(z_i) = \mathbb{e}[\sigma_p(z_i) z_i^2] = 0$  (due to  $\sigma_p(x)$  being an odd function) and  $\mathbb{e}[\sigma_p(z_i) z_i] = \| f_i\|^2 \mathbb{e}[\sigma_p'(z_i)]$ . in (c),  $r_{ij}$  is the remainder term, defined as

$$
\begin{array}{l} r _ {i j} = \left(\boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {j}\right) \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} \left(z _ {i}\right) \left(\mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} \left(\sqrt {1 - \rho_ {i j} \rho_ {j i}} z _ {j}\right) - \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} \left(z _ {j}\right)\right) \\ + \frac {1}{6} \rho_ {i j} ^ {3} \mathbb {e} \left[ \sigma_ {p} \left(z _ {i}\right) z _ {i} ^ {3} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime \prime \prime} \left(\theta_ {i j}\right) \right]. \tag {142} \\ \end{array}
$$

for the case of  $i = j$ , we define  $r_{ii} = 0$ .

using (141), we can verify the following decomposition of  $\sigma_{a}$ :

$$
\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {a} = \left(\mu_ {1, p} \boldsymbol {i} + \boldsymbol {d} _ {1}\right) \boldsymbol {f} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} \left(\mu_ {1, p} \boldsymbol {i} + \boldsymbol {d} _ {1}\right) + \mu_ {2, p} ^ {2} \boldsymbol {i} + \boldsymbol {d} _ {2} + \boldsymbol {d} _ {3} + \boldsymbol {r}
$$

where  $d_{1} = \mathrm{diag}\left\{\mathbb{e}\sigma_{p}^{\prime}(z_{i}) - \mu_{1,p}\right\}$

$$
\boldsymbol {d} _ {2} = \operatorname {d i a g} \left\{\mu_ {1, p} ^ {2} - \| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \| ^ {2} \left[ \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {\prime} \left(z _ {i}\right) \right] ^ {2} \right\},
$$

and

$$
\boldsymbol {d} _ {3} = \operatorname {d i a g} \left\{\mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {2} \left(z _ {i}\right) - \mu_ {1, p} ^ {2} - \mu_ {2, p} ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

since  $\pmb{\sigma}_{b} = \mu_{1,p}^{2}\pmb{f}^{\top}\pmb{f} + \mu_{2,p}^{2}\pmb{i}$ , we must have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {a} - \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {b} \right\| \leq \left(2 \mu_ {1, p} + \| \boldsymbol {d} _ {1} \|\right) \| \boldsymbol {f} \| ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {d} _ {1} \| \tag {143} \\ + \left\| \boldsymbol {d} _ {2} \right\| + \left\| \boldsymbol {d} _ {3} \right\| + \left\| \boldsymbol {r} \right\|. \\ \end{array}
$$

recall the assumptions about the feature vectors in (64). it then follows from (139) that  $\| d_1\| \leq \kappa_p\log p / \sqrt{p}$ . similarly, we also have  $\| d_2\| \leq \kappa_p\log p / \sqrt{p}$ . controlling  $\| d_3\|$  requires a few more steps. let  $z\sim \mathcal{n}(0,1)$  and  $t = \sqrt{2\log p}$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {2} (z _ {i}) - \mu_ {1, p} ^ {2} - \mu_ {2, p} ^ {2} \right| \\ = \left| \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {2} (\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \| z) - \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {2} (z) \right| \\ \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ \left| \sigma_ {p} ^ {2} (\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \| z) - \sigma_ {p} ^ {2} (z) \right| \left(\mathbb {1} _ {| z | > t} + \mathbb {1} _ {| z | \leq t}\right) \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \stackrel {(a)} {\leq} \sqrt {2} \left[ \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {4} (\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \| z) + \mathbb {e} \sigma_ {p} ^ {4} (z) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \sqrt {\mathbb {p} (| z | > t)} \\ + \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p | \| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} \| ^ {2} - 1 | \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\stackrel {(b)} {\leq} \frac {\left(\kappa_ {p} + 1\right) \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}}. \tag {144}
$$

here, (a) uses holder's inequality and the fact that the derivative of  $\sigma_p^2(x)$  is bounded by  $\text{polylog } p$  within the interval

$|x| \leq \max \left\{\|f_1\|, 1\right\} t$ ; (b) applies the standard tail bound  $\mathbb{p}(z > t) \leq 2e^{-t^2 / 2}$ . as (144) holds for all  $i \leq p$ , we have  $\|d_3\| \leq (\kappa_p + 1)\log p / \sqrt{p}$ . the last term to consider is the remainder matrix  $\pmb{r}$ . from its definition in (142), we can easily verify that

$$
\max  _ {1 \leq i, j \leq p} | r _ {i j} | \leq \frac {\kappa_ {p} ^ {3} \operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{p ^ {3 / 2}}.
$$

it follows that  $\| r\| \leq \| r\|_{\mathrm{f}} = \sqrt{\sum_{i,j}r_{ij}^2}\leq$ $\kappa_p^3\mathrm{polylog}p / \sqrt{p}.$  substituting our bounds for  $\| d_1\| ,\| d_2\| ,\| d_3\|$  and  $\| r\|$  into (143), we then reach the bound (140) in the statement of the lemma.

next, we prove an auxiliary result that will be used in the proof of theorem 2. here, we consider a particular sequence of activation functions  $\{\widehat{\sigma}_p(x)\}$  as defined in (110). they form a family of smoothly truncated versions of a fixed activation function  $\sigma(x)$ .

lemma 6. let  $\mu_1, \mu_2$  and  $\mu_{1,p}, \mu_{2,p}$  be the constants associated with  $\sigma(x)$  and  $\widehat{\sigma}_p(x)$ , respectively. if the threshold  $\tau_p = \sqrt{2c_\tau\log p}$  in (110) is chosen with a constant  $c_\tau \geq 2$ , then

$$
\left| \mu_ {1} - \mu_ {1, p} \right| \leq \frac {\operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{p} \quad a n d \quad \left| \mu_ {2} - \mu_ {2, p} \right| \leq \frac {\operatorname {p o l y l o g} p}{\sqrt {p}}. \tag {145}
$$

proof. by construction,  $\sigma(x) = \widehat{\sigma}_p(x)$  and  $\sigma'(x) = \widehat{\sigma}_p'(x)$  for  $|x| < \tau_p$ . let  $z \sim \mathcal{n}(0,1)$ . we then have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mu_ {1} - \mu_ {1, p} \right| \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ \left| \sigma^ {\prime} (z) - \widehat {\sigma} _ {p} ^ {\prime} (z) \right| \mathbb {1} _ {| z | \geq \tau_ {p}} \right] \\ \leq \sqrt {\mathbb {e} (\sigma^ {\prime} (z) - \widehat {\sigma} _ {p} ^ {\prime} (z)) ^ {2}} \sqrt {\mathbb {p} (| z | \geq \tau_ {p})} \\ \leq \frac {\text {p o l y l o g} p}{p}, \tag {146} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last step uses the gaussian tail bound  $\mathbb{p}(|z| \geq \tau_p) \leq 2 / p^2$  for  $\tau_p \geq 2\sqrt{\log p}$ . the same truncation techniques will also give us

$$
\left| \mathbb {e} \sigma^ {2} (z) - \mathbb {e} \widehat {\sigma} _ {p} ^ {2} (z) \right| \leq \frac {\text {p o l y l o g} p}{p}.
$$

combining this bound with (146) and recall the definitions of  $\mu_{2}$  and  $\mu_{2,p}$ , we have  $\left|\mu_2^2 -\mu_{2,p}^2\right|\leq (\mathrm{polylog}p) / p$ . finally, the second bound in (145) can be obtained from the following inequality:  $\left|\sqrt{x} -\sqrt{y}\right|\leq \sqrt{|x - y|}$  for any two nonnegative numbers  $x$  and  $y$ .

# e. some concentration results

# 1) concentration of gaussian vectors:

lemma 7. let  $\mathcal{a}_1$  be the event defined in (22). there exists a constant  $c > 0$  such that

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\mathcal {a} _ {1}\right) \geq 1 - c \exp \left(- (\log p) ^ {2} / c\right).
$$

proof. we start by stating the following simple result: for  $\pmb{f}_1, \pmb{f}_2 \stackrel{i.i.d.}{\sim} \mathcal{n}\left(0, \frac{1}{d} \pmb{i}_d\right)$ , there exists positive constants  $c$  and  $k$  such that for any  $\varepsilon \geq 0$

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\left| \boldsymbol {f} _ {1} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {f} _ {2} \right| \geq \varepsilon\right) \leq 2 \exp \left[ - c d \min  \left\{\frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{k ^ {2}}, \frac {\varepsilon}{k} \right\} \right]. \tag {147}
$$

and

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {f} _ {1} \right\| ^ {2} - 1 \mid \geq \varepsilon\right) \leq 2 \exp \left[ - c d \min  \left\{\frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{k ^ {2}}, \frac {\varepsilon}{k} \right\} \right]. \tag {148}
$$

indeed, for any  $i \in [d]$ ,  $f_{1,i}$  and  $f_{2,i}$  are both sub-gaussian random variables with sub-gaussian norm bounded by  $\frac{c}{\sqrt{d}}$ , for some  $c > 0$  [47, example 2.5.8], so  $f_{1,i}f_{2,i}$  is a subexponential random variable with sub-exponential norm  $\frac{c^2}{d}$  [47, lemma 2.7.7]. then we can apply bernstein's inequality [47, corollary 2.8.3] to get (147). also, (148) can be proved in the same way. then we can let  $\varepsilon = \frac{(\log p)^2}{\sqrt{p}}$  in (147) and (148) and use union bound to get for any  $p$ ,

$$
\mathbb {p} \left(\max  _ {1 \leq i <   j \leq p} \left| \boldsymbol {f} _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {f} _ {j} \right| \geq \frac {(\log p) ^ {2}}{\sqrt {p}}\right) \leq c \exp \left(- (\log p) ^ {2} / c\right) \tag {149}
$$

and

$$
